On `examples/counter_service.verbose` with one line changed to `set count = req.path`, the compiler reported `verified: 0 concept(s), 1 rule(s); all proofs check out`, the emitter's `_ =>` arm ran `emit_eval_expr` on a text field — loading `req.path`'s (ptr, len) *pointer* slot — and stored it into the number slot, after which the handler's `concat("count:", state.count)` **serialised it into the response body**. Measured over three requests: `count:0`, then `count:140733822829620` twice; across three restarts the value was `…017748` / `…681572` / `…246276`, all `0x7ffd…` — a live stack address, different every run, handed to an unauthenticated client over HTTP. **Same family as PR #182's `t.s * 2`, and the difference is the reach**: #182 printed the pointer on the stdout of a CLI binary the operator ran themselves, this one is a listener answering the network. **PRE-EXISTING, not introduced by the text-state slice**: `690f0c9` (before text state) accepts the identical program, so it dates from Number state (PR #47). **The write-path matrix, measured before and after** (13 Number-field sources × 9 Text-field sources): a NUMBER field accepted **every** source — text literal, `req.path` / `req.method` / `req.body`, `concat(...)`, `substring(...)`, `state.<text field>`, a bool expression, and an `if/else` whose arms are text — and three of those reached the emitter as working binaries (`req.path` 844 B, `req.method` 844 B, the `if/else` 909 B), the rest failing later with an unattributable native codegen error. A TEXT field was already refused for every Number/Bool source, but **INCIDENTALLY**: `text_source_worst_case` has no arm for `now_unix()` / `length(...)` / arithmetic, so it reported *"source shape has no compile-time byte bound"* — true, and the wrong diagnosis, since the answer is "wrong type", not "declare a bigger buffer". Fix: route every `set` RHS through `check_expr_against` — the SAME bidirectional checker that already covers a rule's body, every `let` RHS and every binary operand — with `state.<field>` resolved through the EXISTING `bindings` map (a synthetic concept whose fields are the state fields, registered under the name `state`), so `infer_expr_type`'s `Field(Ident(b), f)` arm answers with the declared type and there is no second notion of what `state.x` means. A split is exactly how the hole existed. Errors are re-contexted to `service '<s>' / after / set <f>`, and the bound gate is skipped for a `set` the type check already flagged, so each offence yields ONE attributable error. After: all 22 mistyped cells refused, all 8 correctly-typed cells still accepted. **THE READ PATH IS A SEPARATE, NON-DISCLOSING GAP, measured**: `state.<field>` is invisible to `infer_expr_type` (its base is neither the input name nor a binding), so `state.<text> * 2`, `status: state.<text>`, `starts_with(state.<number>, "a")` and `length(state.<number>)` all still VERIFY — and all four are refused by the emitter (`unknown field 'last'`, `length: argument must be a text literal…`), i.e. they fail closed with no wrong answer and no leak. Closing it means threading service state into `verify_rule`, which is a different plumbing point from `verify_service` and its own slice. Two more accepted-but-emitter-refused residuals, both pre-existing and both fail-closed: an indirection through a handler `let` (`let p = req.path` then `set count = p` → `unresolved identifier 'p'`, because a non-record `Ident` infers `None`), and `set count = req.timestamp` (a log-scope-only synthetic → `unknown field 'timestamp'`). Cost: **zero emitted bytes** — verifier-side, measured over all 155 examples × every rule / service / reaction (**1377 targets, 818 emitted, 559 refused**), compiler from `24e3c7c` vs branch, with the baseline-vs-baseline control EMPTY first: **0 status changes, 0 size changes, 0 same-size-different-bytes, 818/818 identical by size + sha256**. All 155 examples still verify; `counter_service` stays 975 B answering `count:0/1/2` and `last_path_service` 1027 B answering `prev:none / prev:/alpha / prev:/beta`, both driven over real TCP. gen0 refuses every stateful example at every index and always did, so the acceptance figure stays **96/155** and R0/R1/R2 hold. **ONE of the 559 existing tests had grown into the hole**, and it is a finding rather than a fixture to paper over: `service_text_state_refuses_unbounded_source_shape` probed refusal #5 with `now_unix()` on the reasoning that it "is a Number-producing shape with no text bound" — both halves true, conclusion wrong, because that is a TYPE error the bound gate was only reporting for want of anything upstream looking at types. Corrected, not accommodated: the probe is now `json_escape(req.path)`, which is text-typed (so it passes the type check) and deliberately absent from `text_source_worst_case`'s table (so the bound gate is the only thing that can refuse it), and the `now_unix()` case is re-asserted in its new home with an explicit anti-assertion that it is NOT also reported as a missing byte bound. Pinned by `native::tests::state_set_type_mismatch_cannot_serve_a_stack_address`, which asserts the refusal for four shapes (including the `if/else`, which a top-node-only check would miss), then proves the refusal is not vacuous by asking the EMITTER directly — `text_state_drive` goes through `compile_service`, which bypasses `verify_program`, so the leaking binary is still built and driven over real TCP and the served value asserted to be a stack address — then pins both corrected twins end to end; verified to FAIL against `24e3c7c` (`got []`)
- **SECURITY — a fail-closed primitive on REQUEST data `sys_exit(1)`'d the whole LISTENER; one client, one packet, server dead (fixed 2026-09-03, slice `svc-client-abort-1`).** `byte_at`'s index check, `substring`'s two bound checks and `parse_int`'s malformed-input scan each emit an INLINE `mov rax,60 ; mov rdi,1 ; syscall` behind a `jmp .ok` — designed for a one-shot RULE binary, where exiting is free and the exit code IS the contract. Inside a service handler the same 16 bytes are a remote kill switch. **Measured on `5c2377d`** with three one-rule sequential HTTP/1.0 services whose body is `concat("b:", <expr>)`, each sent exactly `GET /ab HTTP/1.0`: `byte_at(req.path, 50)` → rc 1, listener gone (1028 B); `substring(req.path, 0, 50)` → rc 1, gone (1027 B); `parse_int(substring(req.path, 1, 3))` → rc 1, gone (1172 B); the same shape in a `raw_tcp` handler (`byte_at(req.data, 5)` on a 2-byte frame) → rc 1, gone (621 B); and two positions the brief did not list, measured because the handler is not the only place request data is evaluated — `byte_at(req.path, 50)` inside a handler `fetch`'s REQUEST BYTES (slice 11.3 builds them from `req.*`) → rc 1, gone (1269 B), and `parse_int(req.path)` as `log:` CONTENT (the log grammar admits it) → rc 1, gone (1282 B). The repo already refused this shape twice in writing — the HTTP parse's method/path guards route to close-without-response ("an over-long path must not be a remote kill switch"), and `docs/text-state-fields-design.md` §3.3 rejected a runtime `sys_exit(1)` as a listener's primary gate — and slice `rawtcp-inspect-0` (PR #203) inherited the defect because `docs/multistep-connection-design.md` §4.6-3 SAID "exits 1 with no response"; that design was wrong there, its own test pinned the kill switch as the contract, and PR #203's report flagged it. **Service-time aborts are now TWO CLASSES, in both service emitters.** OPERATOR aborts keep `sys_exit(1)`: resource open/read under `on_read_error: abort`, fetch socket/connect/write/read under `on_connect_error: abort`, `log:` `on_error: abort`, the entropy backstop, the text-state backstop — deployment conditions, not client input — and, by PROVENANCE rather than position, `parse_int(read(<resource>))`, whose malformed content is the operator's file (the corpus idiom for a runtime-tunable limit in `audit_gateway` / `body_size_gate` / `body_content_gate` / `uri_size_gate`; first measured moving −18 B each under a site-based rule, then carved out so all four are byte-identical). CLIENT aborts close the CONNECTION: every `byte_at` / `substring` / `parse_int` check emitted while lowering the handler's `let` prologue, its body, the `after:` block's `set` sources, a fetch's request bytes and a log block's content. Sequential: the existing close path (`close(client_fd)` then `lea rsp, [rbp - frame_size] ; jmp accept_top`, which also frees whatever the aborting primitive had pushed); forked: the same path, so the child takes its normal `sys_exit(0)` tail; raw_tcp: the existing `jle close` target. **The mechanism is a SCOPE, not a threaded vector**: the four sites live deep in `emit_eval_expr` and its text helpers, which take no abort vector, and threading one through ~45 call sites would be a refactor with more surface than the defect — so `ClientAbortScope` (a thread-local, RAII-restored on the `?` path) is opened by the service emitter around handler lowering, each site hands its `jcc rel32` offsets to it via `route_client_abort` and emits NOTHING else, and the emitter resolves them to its close label; outside a scope every site is byte-for-byte what it was. **A site whose fd is live in r15 cannot take the bare close path**: a fetch's request bytes are evaluated after `socket` + `connect`, and `emit_append_file_call` opens the log fd before it evaluates the content — those two scopes resolve to a 17-byte `r15 close stub` (`mov rax,3 ; mov rdi,r15 ; syscall ; jmp close_label`), emitted only when such a site exists, and the fd count after a tripped request is 4 in both cases (stdin/out/err + the listening socket — a leak would be 5). **What the scope deliberately does NOT cover**: `abort_if(<expr>)` in a streamed bytes body (an EXPLICIT, declared abort, not an implicit bound), and a `let m = read(r)` then `parse_int(m)` — a handler `let` is opaque at the site, so it lands in the CLIENT class, the SAFE misclassification (listener survives; the reverse would re-open the kill switch). **Cost**: −21 B per `byte_at` / `substring` site (the `jmp .ok` + the inline exit), −18 B per `parse_int` site, +17 B once per service that needs the r15 stub; the three probes go 1028 → 1007, 1027 → 1006, 1172 → 1133 (−21 −18: one substring, one parse_int), raw_tcp 621 → 600, fetch 1269 → 1265, log 1282 → 1281, and every client-only service now carries ZERO `sys_exit(1)` sequences in its ELF — there is no exit(1) for a client to reach. **Additive by measurement**: all 159 examples × every rule / reaction / service (1388 targets, 827 emitted / 561 refused), compiler from `5c2377d` vs branch, baseline-vs-baseline control EMPTY first — **0 status changes, 0 same-size-different-bytes, 1386 identical by size + sha256, and exactly TWO size changes, both services with a client-abortable primitive on request data**: `tag_probe::prober` 627 → 606 (−21, the one `byte_at` on the frame) and `nonce_service::token_server` 3659 → 3323 (−336 = 16 × 21: the sixteen `byte_at(random(nonce), i)` sites, each with a literal index inside the declared 16-byte draw, so each abort is unreachable by construction — the bytes move, the behaviour cannot). Every RULE binary is byte-identical, as the scope's absence guarantees structurally. **Pinned** (all over real TCP, tripping request FIRST so "answered after" is measured on the surviving process): `service_client_abort_closes_connection_and_listener_survives` (the three primitives × http_1_0 sequential: no bytes to the tripping connection, a good request on a NEW connection answered, process alive, fd count 4, zero exit(1) sequences in the ELF), `service_client_abort_in_after_block_keeps_state_and_listener` (the response is already on the wire, the state stays unchanged), `service_client_abort_under_forked_takes_the_child_tail`, `service_client_abort_inside_fetch_request_closes_upstream_fd` (a real upstream `TcpListener`; fd count proves the r15 stub closed the socket), `service_client_abort_inside_log_content_closes_log_fd`, `rawtcp_byte_at_out_of_range_closes_the_connection_and_the_listener_survives` (the rewritten PR #203 pin, tripping frame in the MIDDLE, plus the streaming caveat: a `byte_at` after a streamed literal leaves the client the prefix then the close — the streaming ABI's documented mid-walk divergence, not buffered around), `service_operator_aborts_still_exit_1` (the OVER-FIX GUARD: a missing resource under `on_read_error: abort` and an unopenable `log:` under `on_error: abort` each still exit 1, each still carrying exactly one exit(1) sequence), and `client_abort_scope_is_restored_after_a_refused_service` (a `?`-refused service leaves the thread-local `None`, and the rule binary compiled after it is byte-identical to the one compiled before). **Negative control, run and reverted**: with the handler's client sites resolved back to the operator exit tail, the listener-survival test FAILS on exactly its "listener must survive" assertion (`exit == Some(1)`), so the pin is not vacuous. Docs corrected in the same PR: `docs/multistep-connection-design.md` §4 (a dated correction paragraph), §4.1 item 3, §4.6-3, §4.7 NC-0c and §5.7; `examples/tag_probe.verbose`'s comment; the three primitive bullets above.
- **A named top-level item may not be declared twice (fixed 2026-08-29).** A program declaring `rule f` twice was accepted (`all proofs check out`) and the two backends then SILENTLY DISAGREED about which definition wins: the interpreter resolves a call with `all_rules.iter().find` (FIRST match), the native emitter with `HashMap<name, &Rule>` whose `insert` overwrites (LAST match). Measured on a 3-rule probe (`f`→v+1, `caller`→f, second `f`→v+100): `--run caller` on v=5 gave **6**, `--native` gave **105**, both at rc 0. Worse than the arity/text-arith/record-field gaps in the same family: there BOTH executors ran and gave DIFFERENT plausible answers — a "verified" program with two meanings, the project's central thesis (the verifier is the durable artifact) failing outright. The verifier itself was not immune: its own `all_rules` is a Vec walked with `.find` (first) while its `concepts` HashMap keeps the LAST, so it type-checked a mixed view. Fix: a duplicate-name pre-pass in `verify_program` (`src/verifier.rs`) refusing a second declaration of any `rule` / top-level `concept` / `reaction` / `service` / `concept_group`, breadcrumb naming the offender (`duplicate rule name 'f' (already declared earlier); …`). **Item-kind matrix** (backend behaviour BEFORE the fix): rules — silent, backends disagree; top-level concepts — silent overwrite (caught only incidentally when the surviving def lacked the referenced field); reactions / services / concept-group NAMES — silently accepted. Already refused before this fix (unchanged): `resource` and `connection` names (their own duplicate check; connections also may not collide with a resource), and concept names declared INSIDE a group (checked against the shared concept namespace). Cost: **zero emitted bytes** — verifier-side; the sweep over all 154 examples (1443 rule/service/reaction native targets) was byte-identical baseline-vs-branch with the baseline-vs-baseline control empty first, and the one duplicate in the corpus (`examples/vexprparse.verbose` declared `rule arg_list_len` twice, bodies byte-identical bar `@source`) was deleted — proven byte-neutral because the native emitter dedups rules by name, so gen0 self-compiles to the identical 657831-byte binary (sha256 `ea4a16c1…`) with the duplicate and without it; R0/R1/R2 hold. Not one of the 544 existing tests had grown into the hole. Pinned by `duplicate_top_level_item_name_rejected_at_verify_time` (all five kinds + a renamed twin that must verify clean) and `native::tests::duplicate_rule_name_hazard_is_closed_at_verify` (which demonstrates the 105-vs-6 divergence by bypassing the verifier, then asserts the verifier now refuses it); both verified to FAIL against f2e9a0e (`got []`). gen0 does NOT mirror this check yet — a new gaps-table row (verbosec refuses, gen0 accepts; the safe direction)
- General reduction: `fold(collection, initial, acc, var => body)`
- Proofs: purity (reads/calls), termination (bound, structural, decreasing, increasing)
- Hints: `vectorizable: "reason"`, `parallel: "reason"`, `cache_result: "reason"` (justification required, parser rejects bare form), `overflow: [min, max]` (bounds mechanically verified against interval arithmetic)
- Traceability: `@intention` (string), `@source` (file:line), `@layer: domain|application|interface` (optional, sealed-subgraph discipline)
- Modules: `use "stdlib/finance.verbose"`
- **Service mutable state**: a `service` may declare a `state:` block whose fields persist across requests for the process's lifetime — `count : number = 0` or, since slice `text-state-1` (2026-08-30), `last : text [..256] = "none"`, one field per line, initial value a literal. The handler READS them as `state.<field>` (and must declare `reads : [state.<field>]` in its purity proof, so a stateful handler is greppable exactly like a resource-reading one), and an `after:` block MUTATES them with `set <field> = <expr>`, where the expression may reference `state.*` and `req.*`. **The RHS is TYPE-CHECKED against the field's declared type since 2026-08-30 — it was not, and that was a remotely reachable ASLR disclosure; see the security bullet above.** *(This sentence also said `resp.*`, and no `resp` scope exists in the after block: `infer_expr_type` cannot resolve the base, so `set count = resp.status` verifies silently and the emitter refuses it with `unknown field 'status'` — fail-closed, and a doc claim rather than a feature.)* **The `after:` block runs after the response is written AND after every `log:` block** — so a state field is never observable to the request that changed it, and the audit line records the state the request was SERVED with, not the state it left behind. Worked examples: `examples/counter_service.verbose` (975 B on port 18950; three requests measured as `count:0`, `count:1`, `count:2`) and `examples/last_path_service.verbose` (1027 B; three requests measured as `prev:none`, `prev:/alpha`, `prev:/beta`). Restricted to `Protocol::Http10`, and duplicate field names are refused. **An `after:` mutation together with `concurrency: forked` is REFUSED at verify time (2026-08-30).**

*This bullet said **Number-only** until slice `text-state-1`, citing the two independent refusals at `src/parser.rs` and `src/verifier.rs`. Both are gone; what replaced them is the shape below.* A **text** state field is `<name> : text [..N] = "<literal>"`, with the `[..N]` bound MANDATORY (`1 ≤ N ≤ 65536`) and the initial value a text literal only. Both restrictions are consequences, not preferences: the buffer is allocated once in the service prologue, before any request exists, so its size must be a compile-time constant and there is no runtime-`strlen` sizing fallback the way there is for a concept field; and the init runs above `accept_top`, where there is no request scope and a `read()` would have no error policy to consult. It gets the same `(ptr_slot, len_slot) + N-byte padded buffer` triple in the service's rbp frame that a `resource` and a `connection` already get, and it inherits the persistence property those slots already had for a counter — `lea rsp, [rbp - frame_size]` restores rsp *below* the state block and never touches it, a statement that makes no reference to width. `set` **COPIES** the bytes in with `rep movsb`; it never stores the source pointer. Reads register under the composite key `__state_<field>` and resolve through the existing BoundText machinery, so `concat`, `length` and a direct `HttpResponse.body` work unchanged. Design + options considered: [docs/text-state-fields-design.md](docs/text-state-fields-design.md).

**The overflow gate is COMPILE-TIME, and that is the security argument rather than a performance one.** For each `set <f> = <expr>` the verifier proves `worst_case(expr) ≤ N` from declarations alone — text literal → its byte length, `req.method` / `req.path` → the built-in concept's declared bound, `req.body` → the service's `max_request`, `state.<g>` → g's own N, `read(r)` → the resource's `max:`, `fetch(c, _)` → `max_response:`, a handler text `let` → recursive over its RHS, `substring(t, _, _)` → bound of `t`, `concat(…)` → the sum — or REFUSES. A program that passes emits **zero** check bytes. The gate is sound only because every one of those compile-time bounds is ALREADY runtime-enforced where the bytes enter the process (the HTTP parse's `emit_token_len_guard` for method/path, the kernel's `read` count for body, the buffer size for read/fetch), so the compiler compares a declaration against a declaration — **a stated dependency: weaken any row of that enforcement and this gate breaks silently**, which is why NC-6 below exists. A runtime `sys_exit(1)` was rejected as the PRIMARY gate for a reason specific to this construct: every other fail-closed bound in this compiler lives in a one-shot rule binary where exiting is free, and this one lives inside a **listener**, so a client who could drive the source past `N` would have a one-packet DoS. The emitter had already made exactly that call, in writing, at the HTTP-parse bound guards.

**A 13-byte backstop stays at the copy site** (`cmp rdx, N ; ja <the existing abort tail>`), unreachable by construction given the gate above — and it is a mechanism rather than decoration because two negative controls REACH it. With the compile-time gate patched out, a 61-byte path against a `[..8]` field makes the binary `exit(1)` instead of overflowing; with the `req.path` parse guard ALSO removed (NC-6), a 3001-byte path hits the backstop and exits 1, while removing the backstop too gives a **SIGSEGV**. So the backstop is precisely what stands between "a future regression in the parse guard" and "a remote stack overflow" — the 2026-08-05 defect, remotely.

**The `after:` block's position is now a load-bearing invariant, not an ordering preference.** It runs BEFORE the iteration tail's `lea rsp`, so every transient a `set` may copy FROM — a handler concat buffer, a resource buffer, a fetch response, the HTTP read buffer itself — is still live at the copy point. The copy is expressible for no other reason, and a future slice that moves the free earlier or the after block later breaks it silently (the copy would read freed stack). Recorded at the ITERATION TAIL in `emit_http10_dynamic_bytes`.

**Refused in slice 1, each naming the offender and the lifting slice**: no `[..N]` on a text field; a bound outside `1..=65536`; an initial literal longer than `N`; a non-literal initial value (**text-state-3** lifts `read()`); a `set` source with no compile-time bound; a `set` whose worst case exceeds `N` — **including the append-accumulator `concat(state.f, …)`, which can never satisfy the gate at ANY bound** because `worst_case = N + 256 > N` for every N, so it needs a declared `on_overflow:` policy (**text-state-2**); `state.<f>` in `starts_with` / `ends_with` / `contains` / `substring` / `json_escape` / `parse_int` / text `==`, each naming the emitter arm that would have to grow (**text-state-2**; the workaround is to bind it to a handler `let` first, which works today); a `bytes`-typed state field, which would be **write-only** since a service has no bytes-valued position (`HttpResponse.body` and `req.body` are both text) — **text-state-4**; and `state:` on `raw_tcp`, unchanged. `state:` + `concurrency: forked` is inherited from PR #194, keyed on `after_sets`.

**Byte-neutral by measurement**: sweeping all 154 pre-existing examples × every rule / service / reaction (1375 targets), baseline `690f0c9` vs branch, baseline-vs-baseline control EMPTY first — **1375/1375 identical by size + sha256, 0 status changes, 0 size changes, 0 same-size-different-bytes**, `counter_service` still 975 B at the same hash, and the only new rows are the new example's two. gen0 REFUSES both stateful examples at every index (rc 1, zero bytes) and always did — the refusal is `state:` itself, not text state, so the acceptance figure stays 96 and no gaps-table row moves; gen0's own binary is byte-identical (sha256 `ea4a16c1…`) and R0/R1/R2 hold.

*This bullet used to say a counter under `forked` "counts per-connection, not globally", framing the combination as degraded-but-useful. That was wrong, and measurably so.* `fork()` is per-`accept` and http_1_0 serves exactly ONE request per connection, so every child starts from the parent's **unchanged** slots, runs its `after:` block against its own copy-on-write page, and `sys_exit(0)`s. The parent never re-reads the slot and no sibling ever sees it: the mutation is observed by **nobody, ever**. Measured on a running binary — `examples/counter_service.verbose` answers `count:0 / count:1 / count:2 / count:3` under the default sequential mode, and with `concurrency: forked` added (1133 B) the same four requests answer **`count:0` four times**. That is a write-only declaration — a constant dressed as a counter — not per-connection counting.

Propagating the mutation would require shared memory between processes, which `docs/effect-model.md` refuses on principle (its "Pthreads / shared-memory concurrency" entry: locks need a memory model, and a memory model is a research problem in its own right). So the combination is **unbuildable under the standing effect model, not merely unbuilt**, which is why it is refused at verify time rather than documented as a caveat — a declaration that means nothing is exactly the *false explicitation* this project forbids by name.

The refusal is keyed on `after_sets`, **not** on `state_fields`: a `state:` block with no `after:` block is a per-process CONSTANT, and a constant reads identically under both concurrency modes, so refusing it would reject a valid program — the one direction this project's verifier checks must never move in. Corpus cost is **zero**: `counter_service.verbose` is the only file in `examples/` with a service `state:` block (verified by a line-anchored grep for `^  state:`) and it declares no `concurrency:`. Pinned by `forked_state_mutation_is_refused_and_sequential_still_counts` — which asserts the refusal, then compiles the SAME source through `compile_service` (which bypasses `verify_program`) and drives four real TCP requests to prove the emitter would still ship the write-only binary, then drives the sequential twin to prove it still counts 0/1/2 — and by `forked_service_with_read_only_state_is_still_accepted`, which guards the over-strict direction.
- Reactions: declared side effects with trigger rules; effects today are `print` (to stdout) and `append_file "path" content` (to a file). Path is a string literal at parse time — dynamic paths are refused so the auditor reads every file the program can ever touch.
- String escapes: **two closed sets, and the split is load-bearing.** A `text` literal takes `\n`, `\r`, `\t`, `\\`, `\"` — and nothing else; an unknown escape is a lex error, never a silent pass-through for typos. A `b"..."` **byte-string literal** takes those five **plus `\xNN`** (exactly two hex digits, either case, `\x` or `\X`), which is the only place in the language an arbitrary byte can be named. *(This bullet said `\n \t \\ \"` until 2026-08-17 and was wrong twice over: `\r` joined the text set in Phase 11 slice 1, and `\xNN` has been legal in `b"..."` since the backend-bytes slice.)* **`\xNN` is deliberately NOT in the text set, and cannot be:** `Expr::Text` is a Rust `String`, the lexer builds it with `s.push(ch as char)`, and every consumer — native, interpreter, the WASM data section, and the optimizer's own folds — reads it back with `.as_bytes()`, so a scalar `>= 0x80` would round-trip as **two** UTF-8 bytes in every one of them at once: `byte_at("\x80", 0)` would be 194, and `length("\x80")` would be 2. Self-consistent, and wrong. Text is UTF-8 and says so; bytes are bytes. Malformed forms (`\x`, `\x0`, `\xzz`, `\xg1`) are lex errors in the byte set too — the set stays enumerable, it is not a hole
- **Declared constant byte tables**: `byte_at(b"<bytes>", <index>)` and `length(b"<bytes>")` — the two BYTE-ADDRESSED primitives accept a `b"..."` literal, so a lookup table can be **declared as data** instead of unrolled into control flow. `examples/aes_sbox.verbose` is the worked example: the FIPS-197 S-box was 254 chained `else if` branches compiling to **10967 B**, and is now one indexed read at **833 B** — 13× smaller, byte-exact on all 256 entries against an S-box derived independently in GF(2^8), and still fail-closed out of range. The table needs `\xNN` for a reason that is not cosmetic: `S[82] = 0x00`, so exactly one entry is inexpressible without it. **Why this is not a step toward C.** It adds a *declared constant*, not a computed-offset store: there is no writable indexed aggregate, no address arithmetic, no pointer syntax, no way to take the address of the table or alias it. The reader is the existing `byte_at`, whose bounds check was already fail-closed (`index >= length` → `sys_exit(1)`, and a negative index falls under the same unsigned compare), and the table's length is a compile-time constant the emitter knows exactly — so the check is against a fact, not an assertion. What the language gained is the ability to say "these 256 bytes are the answer"; what it did not gain is anywhere to put a byte at runtime. **Deliberately narrow**: only a LITERAL, and only in those two positions. A bytes-typed *field*, `le32`/`le64`, or a bytes `concat` stays refused — those are runtime byte values with no compile-time length. Every other text primitive (`starts_with`, `contains`, `ends_with`, `substring`, `parse_int`, `json_escape`, text `concat`) still checks its operand against `Type::Text`, so the `bytes`/`text` isolation documented on `Type::Bytes` ("bytes never implicitly convert to or from Text — the isolation is the point") is intact. Optimizer folds `length(b"...")` and `byte_at(b"...", <literal>)`; an out-of-range literal index deliberately does NOT fold, so the runtime check keeps its job. Native and the interpreter agree byte for byte. **Backend reach is decided by the FOLD, not by the operand type**, which is worth stating because the obvious summary is wrong: `length(b"...")` and `byte_at(b"...", <literal index>)` become a plain `Number` in the optimizer, so **every backend handles them** (measured: native / interpreter / WASM all OK). Only the *runtime-index* form reaches a backend as a real `ByteAt`, and there WASM refuses — **pre-existing and nothing to do with bytes**: `wasm.rs` has no `ByteAt` arm at all (catch-all refusal). Measured identically on the TEXT form with the pre-change compiler, so it is not a regression and is not fixed here. *(This bullet also described `codegen.rs` collapsing `ByteAt` and `Length` to the literal `false`; that backend was removed 2026-08-19.)*
- Three backends: interpreter (`--run`), native x86-64 (`--native`), WASM (`--wasm`). *(A fourth, a Rust-source transpiler behind `--compile` / `--emit-rust`, was REMOVED 2026-08-19 — see the note under "Two Execution Modes". Both flags now refuse with a breadcrumb rather than being silently ignored.)*
- Input modes: argv (default), one-shot stdin (--stdin), streaming line-by-line (--stream)

## Writing .intent Prose

The recognized patterns that the AI maps reliably to Verbose constructs (e.g. "for each X, check Y" → `all`, "for each X, compute Y" → `map`, "total of Y over X" → `sum`, etc.) are published in `INTENT.md`. Future sessions should consult it before inventing a pattern, and extend it when a new pattern is agreed upon. `.intent` evolves freely by design, but only within what we have written down — otherwise every `.intent` file depends on improvisation.

## Separation of Concerns

The compiler (verbosec) NEVER generates code. It verifies and compiles. Code generation is the AI's job, done through a separate tool (not part of the compiler). This boundary is non-negotiable:

- **AI** (external, non-deterministic): reads .intent, generates .verbose with proofs and hints
- **verbosec** (internal, deterministic): verifies proofs against AST, compiles to binary

If they're mixed, the verification loses its value. The compiler's credibility comes from being independent of the generation process.

A dedicated intent-to-verbose generation tool is planned as a separate project/script.

## LLVM Strategy

LLVM is NOT the primary backend. Verbose emits machine code directly because:
1. LLVM IR can't express field ranges, overflow bounds, or optimization hints
2. The translation to LLVM IR loses the domain knowledge that makes Verbose unique
3. LLVM adds overhead (prologues, stack protectors, alignment) that Verbose proves unnecessary

LLVM may become an OPTIONAL fallback backend for platforms without a native emitter. But all architecture decisions must keep the direct-emission path viable and primary.

## Two Execution Modes, Two Security Profiles

Security is pillar #1. Each feature is judged by what attack surface it adds, not by whether it is "useful". Under that frame, the compiler exposes two execution modes — not one primary and one fallback, but **two modes with deliberately different surfaces**:

- **Native (small, auditable surface, actively growing)**: x86-64 machine code emitted directly. No libc, no allocator, no tagged values held across non-local control flow, no dynamic dispatch. Grows phase by phase as new constructs land, each extension reviewed against this list. Binaries stay small (500 B–2 KB) and auditable line by line. As of this writing the native path covers scalar rules, reactions with `append_file`, `Result(number|text, text)`, record outputs, text-typed input fields, and `match_result` in the pass-through shape (see the phase table below).
- **WASM (text-computation parity with native)**: same principles as native but on its own track. WASM today handles scalar rules: arithmetic, comparisons, logic, `if/then/else`, number `let`, `Call(callee, [Ident(input)])` (inlined; refused if callee has its own bindings), the binary scalar primitives `abs(x)` / `min(a, b)` / `max(a, b)` (slice W1, branchless via `select`), and the **full text computation surface** (slices W3a + W3b + W3c): `Type::Text` as input field (encoded as `(i32 ptr, i32 len)` params into the module's exported linear memory) and as output (multi-value `(i32, i32)` return), `Expr::Text` literals embedded in the data section starting at offset 1024 (the first 1 KiB stays reserved: bytes 0..16 for future allocator metadata, bytes 16..48 back the itoa scratch region), `concat(...)` of text and number args via a single-pass bump-allocated fill (allocator state lives in a function-local i32 reset on each rule entry, so returned (ptr, len) values are valid until the next call into the module), text-typed `let` bindings (parked in two i32 slots same shape as a text input field), the W3c text primitives `length(text)` (single i32 widened to i64), `starts_with` / `ends_with` / `contains` (inline branchless byte-compare loops with the validator-required `unreachable` after `end loop`; `contains` uses a 6-i32 scratch group that includes inner+outer counters), `parse_int(text)` (helper function with a strict scan + WASM `unreachable` trap on bad input — same fail-closed posture as native's sys_exit(1); handles `i64::MIN` correctly), and `json_escape(text)` (helper function with a 3-result return `(out_ptr, out_len, new_bump_ptr)` so the caller updates its bump pointer; supports `\"`, `\\`, `\n`, `\r`, `\t` plus `\u00XX` for control bytes). Two helper functions appear in the module's function section (after func 0 = rule): itoa (when concat has number args), parse_int (when used), json_escape (when used) — each gets its own type entry. The (ptr, len) ABI mirrors native's BoundText slot convention exactly, so an auditor reads either backend with the same mental model; the bound travels alongside the data and is never derived from a sentinel. Slice W4-Result (2026-05-05) lifts `Result(number, text)` and `Result(text, text)` outputs. ABI: a tagged tuple multi-value return. `Result(number, text)` lands as `(i32 tag, i64 ok_value, i32 err_ptr, i32 err_len)`; `Result(text, text)` as `(i32 tag, i32 ok_ptr, i32 ok_len, i32 err_ptr, i32 err_len)`. Tag is `0` for Ok, `1` for Err; the un-live arm's slots are placeholder zeros, since the contract is "tag picks the live payload" — the host MUST gate decoding on the tag, not trust un-live slots. The if/else inside a Result-typed rule body uses a multi-value WASM blocktype, encoded as a separate type-section entry `() -> (...result tuple...)` whose typeidx the `if` instruction references. Slice W4-MatchResult (2026-05-06) lifts `match_result(...)` at the rule-body top level. Strategy: inline the callee body via the same `emit_wasm_result_body` (callee shares the input concept, so its params double as the caller's), stash the resulting tagged tuple into per-rule locals, then dispatch on tag with a multi-value if/else. The Ok arm copies the stashed ok payload into a fresh local that's registered as a binding shadowing into a `clone()`-extended `binding_shapes` map; the outer ok_body's `Ident(ok_var)` lookups resolve through it. Same pattern for the Err arm with the err (ptr, len) pair. The bound vars cannot leak past the arm because each arm builds its own extended ctx — clone-on-shadow rather than mutate-and-restore. Locals reservation is layout-aware: NumberText shape adds 2 i64 (ok_stash, ok_var) + 5 i32 (tag, err_ptr+len stash, err_var ptr+len); TextText adds 9 i32 (tag, ok+err ptr+len stashes, ok+err ptr+len bindings). The blocktype typeidx introduced by W4-Result also serves the inner if/else dispatching on tag — no new type entry needed. Slice 1 scope: one match_result, top-level only (rejected if nested in if/Ok/Err); `Call(callee, [Ident(input)])` target shape; same input concept; callee has no let bindings; outer rule shape == callee shape. The first three rejections each carry a clear breadcrumb naming the slice and the constraint, pinned by `wasm_w4_match_result_rejects_callee_with_bindings` / `_cross_concept_callee` / `_nested`. Worked end-to-end via `examples/purchase.verbose::discounted_purchase` — Ok arm transforms (`amount * 90 / 100`), Err arm pass-through. Surfaced and pinned a real bug during this slice: `BinOp::GtEq` and `BinOp::LtEq` had SWAPPED opcodes (0x57=`le_s` and 0x55=`gt_s` instead of 0x59=`ge_s` and 0x57=`le_s`); never caught because no prior W1/W3 test ran a `>=`/`<=` rule whose result depended on the comparison. Fixed; pinned at runtime by `wasm_w1_runtime_gteq_lteq_round_trip`. Refused in W3b (workaround in parens): nested concat (use a `let` for the inner concat — text-let RHSes are now legal). Still refused: `Record` / `collection` outputs, `match_result`, `map`/`filter`/`fold`, quantifiers, `read` / `fetch` / reactions / services. Effects (reactions, services, fetch, read, fork) don't port directly — WASM has no syscalls; bringing them in needs a separate WASI vs host-imports design call. The remaining computational features port mechanically — the AST supports them, the emitter just hasn't been written. This asymmetry is known and deliberate: the security-sensitive target is the native ELF, and WASM's growth follows native once we have a stable convention to mirror.
**REMOVED 2026-08-19 — the Rust-source transpiler (`src/codegen.rs`, `--compile` / `--emit-rust`).** It emitted Rust source and shelled out to `rustc`. It is recorded here rather than deleted silently, because the reasoning generalises to any future "extra backend" proposal.

What made it removable was not that it was broken, though it was: measured over `examples/`, **125 of 152 programs produced Rust that did not compile** (E0308 / E0610 / E0061 / E0425 / E0106), so `rustc` reported errors in generated code the author never wrote. What made it removable is that **nothing depended on it**. Every `codegen`/`emit_rust` hit outside `main.rs` was a comment or the unrelated phrase "native codegen"; it appeared in no CI job; and its one consumer was the `--benchmark` size column.

**And that column was measuring the wrong thing.** It invoked `rustc <file> -o <out>` with no flags — an unoptimized debug build, with assertions and symbols — and printed it beside hand-emitted machine code with no debug info and no libc. Measured on `examples/invoices.verbose`: no flags **3 999 544 B**, `-O` **3 911 248 B**, `opt-level=z + panic=abort + strip + lto` **327 640 B**, native **683 B**. So the table inflated the comparison ~12x: the honest ratio is **~480x, not ~5700x**. The conclusion never depended on the inflation, which is exactly why the straw baseline was worth removing rather than defending.

The serious benchmark, `tools/benchmark.sh`, calls itself the "HONEST benchmark", writes **hand-written equivalents in each language**, and never called the transpiler at all — so the weaker of the two benchmarks was its only consumer.

**The portability argument, stated so it is not re-litigated from memory.** The transpiler was the only path to a native binary off x86-64 Linux (via `rustc`'s targets). That argument is real but was weak in practice: it worked for 27 of 152 programs and nothing in the repo used it that way. WASM covers hosted portability. If a non-x86-64 native target is ever wanted, the answer is an emitter argued on its own merits — not a source-to-source escape hatch that has to be kept in sync with every language change. That sync cost was the standing tax: every new primitive had to consider a fourth backend, and this file is full of "the Rust transpiler emits/refuses X" notes that had to be written and maintained.

Both flags now **refuse with a breadcrumb** naming the alternatives, rather than being silently ignored — the same "closed set, rejected not skipped" rule this project applies to attributes.

- **Interpreter (rich surface)**: the full language — collections, `map`/`filter`/`fold`, all `Result` / `Record` / `match_result` compositions, `@layer`. Runs in a Rust binary that parses JSON and evaluates expressions. Wider surface than native (JSON parser, allocator) but **every expression is still verified by the same compiler** against the same proofs.

Both modes verify the same AST with the same proofs. A rule accepted by the compiler is safe under both modes; only the execution profile differs. Native's trustworthiness comes from careful accumulation — adding a construct is a deliberate commit, never a drive-by "it's missing". Forcing native to grow to "completeness" (full heap, tagged unions, etc.) would add a C-sized attack surface and defeat the point. When native rejects an expression today, the answer is either "add a phase for it under the evolution rules below" or "run it in the interpreter" — never "silently upgrade native to handle it".

## Native Backend Evolution

Tracking what native emits today, what it still rejects, and the design rules that shape how it grows.

### What native emits today

| Phase | Shape | Typical binary | Milestone example |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0 | Scalar rule (`bool` / `number` output from arithmetic, comparisons, field reads) | ~500 B | `invoices.verbose` |
| 1A | Reaction with `append_file "literal_path" "literal_content"` | ~460 B | `audit_simple.verbose` |
| 1B | Reaction with `append_file "literal_path" concat(...)` — dynamic text via inline itoa + stack buffer. Text-field args (e.g. `concat("user=", p.user, ...)`) sized at runtime via per-field `strlen`; `r9` saves the pre-allocation `rsp` so the buffer is freed via `mov rsp, r9` (3 bytes) after the write. Same path also serves `Result(text, text)` Ok/Err arms that concat a text field. | ~720 B (numbers-only) / ~850 B (with text fields) | `audit_log.verbose` (numbers) / `audit_user.verbose` (text field) |
| 2A | Rule with `output: Result(number, text)` — Ok→stdout, Err→stderr, continuation-passing leaves | ~700 B | `purchase.verbose::validate_purchase` |
| 2B | Rule with `output: Result(text, text)` — Ok(text) writes to stdout (literal or concat); shared `emit_text_write_to_fd` helper | ~600 B | `tier.verbose::classify_tier` |
| 2C | Rule with `output: Named(concept)` (record) — JSON serialization to stdout, one object per record. Streaming emission (no on-stack record). Number/text fields supported; `if/else` between two record arms via continuation-passing. Text fields accept literal / input-field / `concat(...)` values (concat uses the Phase 1B dynamic buffer when text-field args are involved). | ~1 KB (basic) / ~760 B (with concat-text) | `classify.verbose::classify_invoice` / `fullname.verbose::compose_greeting` |
| 2E | Text-typed input fields readable in record outputs — argv pointer stored at the rbp slot, length recovered via `repne scasb` (`emit_strlen`) at each read site. | ~600 B | `greeting.verbose::make_report` |
| 2D | `match_result(callee(input), v => Ok(<arith using v>), e => Err(e))` — inlined-callee form. Callee's logic is walked and its Ok/Err leaves are redirected: Ok values bind to a reserved `match_slot` then evaluate the outer Ok arm; Err values write directly to stderr (Err pass-through). Restricted to same-input-concept callees. Pass-through Err arm now routes through the Phase 2F slot path (negligible size overhead vs. the direct-write shortcut). | ~760 B | `purchase.verbose::discounted_purchase` |
| 2F | `match_result` outer Err arm can **transform** the callee's Err value. Two rbp slots (`err_ptr_slot`, `err_len_slot`) represent the bound err_var as a (ptr, len) pair — uniform shape for literals, input-field texts, and concat outputs (the latter aren't NUL-terminated). A third slot (`err_frame_save_slot`) captures pre-capture rsp so any concat buffer the callee's Err allocated gets freed at the end of the outer Err arm via `mov rsp, [rbp+err_frame_save_slot]`. Outer Err body can be any `Err(<text_expr>)` — literal, field, Ident(err_var), or concat mixing any of those. See "Phase 2F design (locked)" below. | ~700 B | `enrich.verbose::enriched` |
| 2G | Text-returning rule call inlined in `emit_text_write_to_fd`. When a text-position expression is `Call(helper, [Ident(input)])`, the emitter recurses on `helper.logic.value` — byte-for-byte equivalent to inlining the helper's body. Same-concept / same-input-name / no-lets restrictions mirror Phase 2D. Unlocks one new code path that flows into every existing text sink (`output: text`, Record field, match_result Err, Result(text,_) arms). See "Phase 2G design (locked)" below. | ~529 B | `compose.verbose::name_line` |
| 2H-a | Same Phase 2G inlining applied to reaction `append_file` content. `emit_append_file_call` factored into `emit_append_write_to_r15`, which recurses on `callee.logic.value` for the Call case with the same restrictions as 2G. The reaction's `open` / `close` bookkeeping stays around the recursion. | ~655 B | `log_via_helper.verbose::log_alert` |
| 2H-b | `Call` as a `concat(...)` argument. Pre-eval loop reserves a `16*N` slot array pointed to by `r11`, evaluates each Call exactly once, stashes `(rax=ptr, rdx=len)` at `[r11 + 16*i + {0,8}]`. Sizing reads `[r11+16*i+8]`; filling copies from `[r11+16*i]`. Final `mov rsp, r9` frees everything. Nested inner concat (callee body = concat) uses `is_nested=true` to skip its own r9 save and refuse further CallText (one level of pre-eval). See "Phase 2H-b design (locked)" below. | ~560–780 B | `compose.verbose::greeting` (772 B) |
| 2I | Non-literal text `let` bindings in `output: text` rules. The prologue's let-eval loop classifies each RHS as text (Concat / text-typed Field / text-returning Call / Ident pointing at a prior text let) or number (everything else). Text bindings get two consecutive rbp slots (ptr, len) — same shape as Phase 2F's err_var — and are registered in a `TextBindings` map carried on `RecordLoopCtx`. The text-output emitter passes that map to `emit_text_write_to_fd`, so `Ident(let-name)` resolves as a BoundText wherever it appears in the logic body or in a later let RHS. The record-loop epilogue's `mov rsp, rbp; pop rbp` frees any concat buffer the lets allocated, once per record. | ~960 B | `ledger_line.verbose::format_line` (964 B) |
| 2I-R | Same as 2I, extended to Result rules (`Result(number, text)` and `Result(text, text)`). `ctx.text_bindings` is threaded through `emit_eval_result_expr` → `emit_match_result_inlined` → `emit_redirect_callee_leaves` so `Ident(let-name)` resolves in Ok, Err, and match_result Err-capture arms. Phase 2F's err_var binding now MERGES with the caller's text_bindings (one clone + insert), letting the outer Err body reference both prior text lets and the captured err_var in the same concat. Scope still pending: collection/fold contexts. | ~750 B | `gate_result.verbose::gate` (750 B) |
| 2I-H | Phase 2I extended to HTTP service handlers. The single rejection at `analyze_http10_handler_shape` ("let bindings in the handler body are not supported until slice 3d+") is removed; handlers with bindings force-route through the Dynamic shape (the Constant fast path doesn't emit a let prologue, so it would silently drop them). `emit_http10_dynamic_bytes` grows `frame_base_fixed` by `n_text_lets * 16 + n_number_lets * 8`, then emits the let prologue between the connection-fetch loop and `emit_handler_to_slots`: text lets call `emit_text_produce_ptrlen` (already serving Phase 2H-b) and store (ptr, len) into two dedicated slots; number lets call `emit_eval_expr` and store the value into one slot. Slot cursor descends from just below the fixed handler block (`-(56 if uses_timestamp else 48) - 8`), so layout never collides with the resource/connection blocks below. Text lets register in `http_text_bindings`; number lets extend a CLONE of `offsets` (called `handler_offsets`) so the original input-field map stays available to the log scope, which deliberately does NOT see handler lets — log content keeps its closed grammar (req/resp fields only). | ~1.1 KB | `greeting_service.verbose::greet` (1081 B) |
| 3 | `output: collection(T)` with `map` or `filter` — streaming element emission (one JSON Lines per element), no arena, count-prefixed argv. `filter` uses identity pass-through: predicate false skips emission, predicate true emits the element as-is. See "Phase 3 design (locked)" below. **Slice 9.5c extension**: a `map` body's record field can use `read(<resource>)`. The resource is opened/read/closed ONCE above the outer record loop (`emit_resource_read_sequence`, same helper as slice 9.1/9.2/9.5); (ptr, len) registered in a local `text_bindings` map and threaded through `emit_record_as_json` (new `text_bindings` param) and `emit_eval_record_expr`. Frame grows by `16 + max_padded` per resource; abort label appended at end of binary (zero-byte cost when no resource is referenced). | ~670 B (no resource) / ~970 B (with read in field) | `payroll.verbose::compute_bonuses` / `tagged_bonuses.verbose::tag_employees` (970 B) |
| 3.2 | `output: collection(number)` / `collection(text)` — scalar element map. `map(w.employees, e => e.salary)` emits one number per line; text body emits one string per line. No JSON wrapping, so scalar-element binaries are smaller (~400-500 B). | ~455 B | `payroll.verbose::salaries` / `::names` |
| 4 | `output: number` with `fold`/`sum`/`count`/`min`/`max` at the top level — inner loop accumulates into a single stack slot, emits the final value on stdout once per input record. First emitter with cross-iteration state; no arena (the accumulator is one i64). See "Phase 4 design (locked)" below. | ~490–530 B | `payroll.verbose::total_salaries` (sum, 486 B) / `::high_earner_count` (count, 532 B) |
| 5a | `output: text` with a per-record body — literal, input text field, or `concat(...)`. One `write` to stdout + newline per input record; no accumulator, no state carried across iterations. Routes to `emit_text_program`, which reuses `emit_text_write_to_fd` (already serving Phase 2B's Ok-text arm). Fold-over-collection to text is Phase 5b. | ~320 B (literal) / ~330 B (field) / ~560 B (concat) | `greeting_line.verbose` (concat, 564 B) |
| 5b | `output: text` via top-level `fold` — appends into a text accumulator over a collection. Body is strictly append-only: `Concat(Ident(acc), ...rest)` with `acc` absent from `rest`. Two-pass emission (pass 1 sums per-element static + `strlen` per text-field arg into rax; pass 2 fills the buffer). `mov r9, rsp; sub rsp, rax` reserves, `mov rsp, r9` frees. See "Phase 5b design (locked)" below. **Slice 9.5 extension**: init may be `read(<resource>)` in addition to a text literal. The resource is opened/read/closed ONCE above the outer loop (same shape as the `emit_record_loop_prologue` path); (ptr, len) live in rbp slots; the per-record init copy uses the runtime length via `mov rsi, [rbp+ptr]; mov rcx, [rbp+len]; rep movsb`. Buffer sized at the resource's `max:` bound (worst case) so a short file produces a tight write but the buffer is never overrun. Failure routes to a shared sys_exit(1) abort label appended at the end of the binary (zero cost when init is literal). **Slice 9.5b extension**: body concat may include `read(<resource>)` args alongside literals/numbers/element fields. The classifier accepts BoundText only when the arg is `Expr::Read` (Ident-bound text and Fetch stay refused — narrower than 9.5c collection scope). Sizing pass adds `r.max_bytes` (compile-time constant) to `static_per_element`, preserving single-pass fold sizing; fill pass reuses the existing `emit_concat_fill` BoundText branch (already handles Read). All referenced resources (init AND body) share one prologue walk via `collect_rule_read_names`. | ~700 B (literal init, no body read) / ~930 B (read init OR body read) | `roster.verbose::roster_line` (708 B) / `banner_roster.verbose::banner_line` (927 B, init read) / `sep_roster.verbose::sep_line` (~924 B, body read) |

| 6 | Scalar output (`number`/`bool`) with embedded quantifiers — `if all(xs, p) then X else if any(xs, p) then Y else Z`. Quantifiers are extracted from the expression tree, desugared to folds, and computed in a **single pass** with one accumulator slot per fold. After the inner loop, the remaining scalar expression is evaluated against the fold results. Multi-accumulator design means N quantifiers = 1 pass, not N passes. | ~700 B | `logs.verbose::health_score` (702 B) / `report.verbose::risk_score` (702 B) |
| stdin | `--stdin` flag prepends a stdin reader prologue (~173 B) that reads whitespace-separated tokens from fd 0, tokenizes, and builds an argc/argv layout on the stack so the rule prologue works unchanged. Enables `echo "data" \| ./binary` and `./binary < file.txt`. Adds ~173 B overhead to any phase's binary. Design in `docs/stdin-reader-design.md`. | +173 B | any example with `--stdin` |
| stream | `--stream` flag wraps the rule code in a line-by-line read loop. Reads ONE line from stdin per iteration (byte-by-byte until `\n`), tokenizes, processes, loops. On EOF, exits cleanly. First long-running Verbose binary. Not supported with SIMD-vectorized or parallel rules. | ~770+ B | `alert.verbose::should_alert` (772 B) |
| 7 (3a–3c) | Top-level `service` construct with `Protocol::Http10` / `Protocol::RawTcp`. Slice 3a synthesises the built-in `HttpRequest` / `HttpResponse` concepts at verify time. Slice 3b emits a constant-response binary (handler logic = a literal `HttpResponse` record). Slice 3c emits the dynamic router: a per-accept loop with HTTP parse → handler → HTTP serialize, with the handler's if/else chain producing the response slots. Same shared register convention as the rest of native (r12=server fd, r14 unused here, rbp frame for handler I/O). | ~430 B (constant) / ~1 KB (router) | `hello_http.verbose` / `hello_router.verbose` |
| 7 (3d) | Handler body assembled at runtime via `concat(...)` of literals, request text fields (`req.method`, `req.path`), and numbers. The concat runs through the existing `emit_concat_to_buffer` path (same code serving reaction logs and `Result(text, _)` arms), leaving `(rax=ptr, rdx=len)` which we store in the body slots `[rbp-32]`/`[rbp-40]`. Iteration epilogue frees the buffer — and any log buffer stacked above it — via `lea rsp, [rbp - frame_size]` (7 bytes) right before `jmp accept_top`. Works because nothing between `accept` and the handler touches `rsp`, so pre-handler `rsp` is always `rbp - frame_size`. | ~1.3 KB | `echo_path.verbose::echo_server` (1263 B) |
| 7 (3e) | `status` field accepts any Number-typed expression inside one HttpResponse record (not just a literal). Slice 3c forced you to wrap in `if … then HttpResponse{200,…} else HttpResponse{404,…}` even when the body was identical; 3e lets `HttpResponse { status: if cond then 200 else 405, body: … }` stand alone. Native trusts the verifier's type-check against the declared `status: number [100, 599]` range and dispatches non-literal status through `emit_eval_expr` → `mov [rbp-24], rax`. Number-literal status keeps the 7-byte immediate-store fast path. | ~900 B | `method_guard.verbose::guard_endpoint` (921 B) |
| 8 (8a–8c) | Per-request `log:` block on a service. Closed grammar: `text`/`number` literals, `concat(...)`, plus `req.method`, `req.path`, `req.timestamp`, `resp.status`, `resp.body`. Slice 8a wires the `append_file` between handler and serializer using shared `emit_append_file_call` (same path as reaction logs). Slice 8b enriches the log scope with `resp.status` (number, slot -24) and `resp.body` (text via BoundText, slots -32/-40) — handler-populated, no extra runtime cost. Slice 8c adds `req.timestamp` (number, slot -56) backed by one `clock_gettime(CLOCK_REALTIME)` after `accept` — frame grows by 8 only when timestamp is referenced. The handler never sees these names; the rewrite is local to the log scope so the response stays reproducible from `(method, path)`. | ~1.3 KB (no ts) / ~1.7 KB (with ts) | `hello_router_logged.verbose` (1294 B) / `audit_complete.verbose` (1735 B) |
| 8 (8d) | Optional `on_error: drop \| abort` line in the log block. Drop is the default (slice 8a behaviour, silent on failure). Abort emits a `test rax, rax; js abort_label` (8 bytes) after each fallible log syscall — open and write — branching to a shared `mov rax, 60; mov rdi, 1; syscall` epilogue (16 bytes) at the end of the binary. Cost: zero when policy is Drop (no checks, no label). Lets the operator opt into fail-closed audit semantics when an Article 12 chain requires that no log persisted means no claim of having served the request. Reaction effects (rules) keep the Drop default; the knob is service-level only. | ~1.2 KB | `audit_strict.verbose::strict_endpoint` (1240 B) |
| 8 (8e) | Multiple `log:` blocks per service. AST: `Service.log: Option<Effect>` + `log_on_error: ErrorPolicy` consolidated into `Service.logs: Vec<LogBlock>` where `LogBlock { effect, on_error }`. Parser pushes per `log:` block instead of overwriting; verifier walks the Vec, applying the closed log-scope grammar block-by-block; native iterates `for log_block in &service.logs` between the handler and the HTTP serialize, calling `emit_append_file_call` once per block with that block's own on_error. The synthetic log scope (req.method/path/timestamp, resp.status/body slots) is built ONCE outside the loop — every block reads the same slot values, so timestamp's `clock_gettime` still fires once per accept regardless of block count, and `uses_timestamp` becomes a Vec union (`logs.iter().any(...)`). Order of declaration is load-bearing for fail-closed semantics: a strict block declared first aborts the binary BEFORE later best-effort blocks emit. Single-log services compile byte-for-byte identically (purely additive slice). | +~340 B per added block | `dual_log.verbose::dual_logged` (1657 B; one strict JSONL audit + one best-effort metrics ndjson) |
| 9 (slice 1) | Top-level `resource <name>` declaration + `read(<name>)` expression. The path is a literal embedded inline (auditor sees every file the binary can open); `max:` bounds a per-resource stack buffer. `emit_record_loop_prologue` walks the rule's logic for `Read` references, allocates `(ptr_slot, len_slot)` + buffer per unique resource, and emits `open(O_RDONLY) → test+js abort → mov r15, rax → read → store len → test+js abort → close` ONCE per rule invocation (before `loop_top`, so per-record loops don't reread). `text_bindings` registers `name → (ptr, len)` so `Expr::Read` reuses the Phase 2I/2F BoundText path through `emit_text_write_to_fd` / `emit_concat_to_buffer`. Failure routes to a per-rule abort label (sys_exit 1). Slice 1 covers `output: text` rules; collection / fold / record / service-handler contexts still reject. | ~540 B | `read_config.verbose::load_config` (541 B) |
| 9 (slice 2) | `read(<name>)` inside an HTTP service handler body. Same `emit_resource_read_sequence` helper as slice 1, hoisted into the per-accept iteration of `emit_http10_dynamic_bytes` (right after `accept` + the optional `clock_gettime`, before HTTP parse + handler dispatch). `frame_base` grows by `sum(16 + max_padded)` per resource, the read buffer + parser scratch shift below. Resource (ptr, len) registered in a local `text_bindings` threaded through `emit_handler_to_slots`; the body field accepts `Expr::Read(name)` directly (loads (ptr, len) into `[rbp-32]/[rbp-40]`) AND inside `concat(...)` via the existing BoundText classifier path. Per-accept, not per-binary-lifetime: the operator can edit the file and the next request sees the new content. Failure shares the slice 8d abort label. Composes with 3e status, 8b/8c log fields, 8d on_error. | ~1.5–1.6 KB | `static_file_server.verbose::static_server` (1572 B) |
| 10 | Service-level `concurrency: forked` opt-in. Default `Sequential` keeps every prior service binary byte-for-byte identical (purely additive slice). When `Forked`: a one-shot `rt_sigaction(SIGCHLD, SIG_IGN, NULL, 8)` (kernel-ABI 32-byte struct inlined via jmp-over-data) runs before the `listen` syscall — kernel auto-reaps children, no `wait`/`waitpid`, no zombies, zero per-request bookkeeping. Then after each `accept` saves client_fd: `mov rax, 57; syscall` (fork), `test rax, rax`, `js fork_error` (write `"fork failed\n"` to stderr + close client_fd + jmp accept_top), `jz child` (fall through to existing iteration body), parent path closes client_fd + `jmp accept_top`. The iteration body is shared — child falls through; parent skips. At the iteration tail, a `match service.concurrency` swaps the existing `lea rsp + jmp accept_top` for `mov rax, 60; mov rdi, 0; syscall` so the child exits 0 instead of looping. r12 (server fd) survives across fork by kernel guarantee; r15 (resource fd from slice 9.2) is allocated in the child after fork, so no parent/child conflict. Restricted to `Protocol::Http10` (verifier rejects forked raw_tcp). | +~160 B | `static_file_server.verbose` with `concurrency: forked` (1730 B) |
| 11 (slice 3) | Connection fetches REORDERED to run AFTER HTTP parse so the request bytes can compose with `req.method` / `req.path`. The handler body's `fetch(upstream, concat(req.method, " ", req.path, " HTTP/1.0\r\n\r\n"))` now produces an upstream call that mirrors the incoming request — a real reverse proxy. Native: the per-accept `for c in &referenced_connections { ... }` loop in `emit_http10_dynamic_bytes` moves from before-parse to after-parse. The literal-only guard in `emit_connection_fetch_sequence` becomes opt-in via a new `allow_dynamic_request: bool` parameter (rule prologue passes `false` to keep slice 11.1 invariants; HTTP service caller passes `true` plus the post-parse offsets so `req.method`/`req.path` resolve in the request_expr). Resource reads stay before parse (their paths are static). Forked-mode unaffected (children inherit the new ordering). Cached resources unaffected (still hoisted above accept_top). One-fetch-per-connection-per-handler still verifier-enforced. | ~1.1 KB | `reverse_proxy.verbose::proxy_server` (1133 B) |
| 11 (slice 2) | `fetch(name, "...")` inside HTTP service handler bodies. Same `emit_connection_fetch_sequence` helper as slice 11.1, hoisted into the per-accept iteration of `emit_http10_dynamic_bytes` (right after the per-accept resource block, before HTTP read). `connection_extra_bytes` extends `frame_base` so per-connection (ptr, len, buffer) slots are below the resource block. r15 reused as outbound socket fd, freed by close before the next iteration. The body field accepts `Expr::Fetch(name, request_expr)` directly via the same BoundText path slice 9.2 wired for `Expr::Read`. Composes with read+fetch in the same handler (resource fd closed before fetch fd opens). Same constraints as slice 11.1: literal-or-concat-of-literals request bytes (slice 11.3 lifts), one fetch per connection per handler, on_connect_error: abort. | ~1.0 KB | `api_gateway.verbose::gateway` (1011 B) |
| 11 (slice 1) | Outbound TCP via `connection <name>` declaration + `fetch(name, request_bytes)` primitive returning `text`. The first OUTBOUND syscall family. New `Item::Connection(Connection)` AST variant; new `Expr::Fetch(name, request_expr)`. Connection block declares `host: "X.X.X.X"` (IPv4 dotted-quad literal — no DNS, no IPv6, no `localhost`), `port: 1..=65535`, `max_response: 1..=64MiB`, `on_connect_error: abort`. Verifier rejects domain names, port out of range, duplicate connection name, `Expr::Fetch` referencing an undeclared connection, or absence from rule's `reads:` proof. Native: `emit_connection_fetch_sequence` runs above `loop_top` (once per rule invocation): socket(AF_INET, SOCK_STREAM, 0) → js abort → mov r15, fd → connect(r15, &sockaddr_in, 16) → js abort → write(r15, request_ptr, request_len) → js abort → read(r15, response_buf, max_response) → js abort → close(r15). Inline 16-byte sockaddr_in literal via jmp-over-data: family=AF_INET, port=htons(literal), addr=inet_aton("X.X.X.X") in network byte order, 8 bytes pad. Response (ptr, len) registered in `text_bindings` so `Expr::Fetch` resolves through the BoundText path. Slice 1 restriction: one fetch per connection per rule, request bytes must be a literal-or-concat-of-literals (no per-record dynamic body), rules-only (service handlers in slice 11.2). | ~620 B | `health_check.verbose::check_health` (623 B) |
| 9 (slice 4) | Opt-in `cache: <true\|false>` on resource declarations (default `false` — purely additive slice, byte-for-byte backward compat). When `true`, the resource's open/read/close sequence is hoisted ABOVE the `accept_top` label in `emit_http10_dynamic_bytes` (between `LISTEN` and `accept_top`) — runs ONCE at server startup. The (ptr_slot, len_slot) populated by that read sit within the prologue-allocated frame and survive every iteration's `lea rsp, [rbp - frame_size]` epilogue. Trade-off: ~3 µs syscall work saved per request, at the cost of staleness (operator edits to the file are NOT picked up until restart). With `concurrency: forked`, the cached read happens once in the parent before the accept loop; children inherit the populated buffer slot via fork's copy-on-write — zero per-child read cost. Best-case efficiency for static assets on a forking server. For rules (non-service): `cache: true` is a syntactic no-op since `emit_record_loop_prologue` already reads above `loop_top` (once per rule invocation). Cached-read failures share the same end-of-binary `sys_exit(1)` abort label as the per-iteration path. | ~1.7 KB (forked + cached + log + timestamp) | `static_file_server.verbose` with `cache: true` (1730 B) |
| A (slice 4.1) | Pattern match on an inlined callee, scalar output. `match callee(input): VariantName(b1, b2) => scalar_body ; ...` where callee is a same-input-concept rule whose `output: Named(C)` and whose body is a chained if/else of `VariantConstruct` leaves. Native generalizes Phase 2D match_result inlining to N-arm sum types: `emit_redirect_variant_leaves` walks the callee's body, at each `VariantConstruct(C, V, fields)` leaf locates the matching arm, builds a substituted arm body (rename callee.input → outer.input in each field expression, then substitute each `Some(binder)` name in arm.body), and emits eval of the substituted body → rax → jmp common_end. The forward-patch list converges every leaf to the same exit point, after which the standard scalar print + jmp loop_top runs. **No prologue change** — pure AST substitution, zero new frame slots, callee never instantiates a Variant value in memory. Slice constraints: scrutinee = `Call(callee, [Ident(input)])` with `arg == outer.input_name`; callee body uses only `If` / `VariantConstruct`; arm bodies stay scalar (Number/Bool). Both Number and Text payload binders work — the substitution model is type-agnostic; the substituted Field expression resolves through emit_eval_expr's existing typed paths. | ~730 B | `token_classify.verbose::score` (732 B) |
| A (slice 4.2) | Pattern match in a text-producing context: `output: text` rule whose logic is `match callee(input): ...` with each arm body producing text (literal, `concat(...)`, or any shape emit_text_write_to_fd accepts). Mirrors slice 4.1 but each redirected arm calls `emit_text_write_to_fd` (writing directly to stdout) instead of `emit_eval_expr` (leaving rax). The new `emit_redirect_variant_leaves_text` helper uses the same AST substitution as 4.1 — text-typed binders flow through `concat(...)` arms via the standard BoundText path. Same slice constraints as 4.1 (same-input-concept callee, callee body of If/VariantConstruct only). Together, slices 4.1 + 4.2 cover the two main destination shapes a Phase A `match` would land in. | ~950 B | `token_label.verbose::label_of` (953 B) |
| A (slice 5.0) | **Safety floor — no new capability.** Detects cycles in the native call graph reachable from the entry rule's logic and refuses with a clear breadcrumb instead of stack-overflowing the compiler. Recursive rules previously crashed `verbosec` at compile time via infinite inlining; after this slice they exit with a message pointing at slice 5.1+. Helpers `detect_native_recursion` / `dfs_native_cycle` / `collect_native_callees`. Symmetric with classic SCC detection, simplified to just emit a witness name for the breadcrumb. | +0 B (refusal path) | n/a (interpreter via `--run` for recursive rules) |
| A (slice 5.1a) | **First real `call <label>` / `ret` instruction in any Verbose native binary.** Lifts slice 5.0's refusal for the tightest shape: direct self-recursion, single-Number-field input, scalar (Number/Bool) output, call graph exactly `{ self }`, no let bindings, no concat / text-let in body. Recursive call's argument must be `Ident(input_name)` (pass-through) — Record materialization is slice 5.1b. ABI: rdi=value, rax=return. Binary layout: leading `jmp .start_actual` skips over the callable; callable lives at offset 5 with `push rbp / mov rbp, rsp / sub rsp, 8 / spill rdi / body / mov rsp, rbp / pop rbp / ret`; `_start` loop loads field into rdi and `call`s. New `SelfCallCtx` struct threaded as 8th parameter through `emit_eval_expr` (43 call sites, default `None`). MANDATORY stderr breadcrumb at every recursive compile: declared `bound:` is NOT a termination proof for recursion until Phase C. Compiler axiom protected — never silently accepts a recursive rule. | ~720 B | (test rule `recurse(n)` in `phase_a5_recursive_compile_and_refuse`) |
| (prereq) | **Runtime input bounds-check at field-load.** Fields declared `[min, max]` now sys_exit(1) on out-of-range input before the rule body runs. Surfaced by subagent review of the slice-5.1+ design doc (point 5): the optimizer assumes declared ranges hold (for interval arithmetic / dead-branch elimination) but the runtime didn't enforce them. Concrete consequence pre-fix: a recursive rule on `v : number [0, 10]` invoked with `v=1000000` produced 1M+ recursive frames before SIGSEGV. After: same case exits(1) at field-load with no rule body entered. New `emit_bounds_check` emits `mov r10, imm64 ; cmp rax, r10 ; jl/jg .abort` after each `emit_atoi_inline` for bounded Number fields. Shares the existing `resource_abort_patches` tail (single sys_exit(1) at end-of-binary). +38 bytes per bounded Number field. Fields without explicit range stay unclamped (purely additive). Prerequisite to slice 5.1b — with bounds enforced, recursion depth has a meaningful audit-defensible bound (input range ⇒ max stack). | +~38 B/bounded-field | n/a (orthogonal safety) |
| (security) | **Runtime input bounds-check at field-load, TEXT half** (2026-08-05). The symmetric other half of the `[min, max]` Number check above — and, until it landed, an **argv-controlled stack buffer overflow**. A field declared `name : text [..N]` is EXPLOITED for compile-time buffer sizing (`emit_concat_to_buffer_impl`'s `static_total += max_len`, its `2 * max_len` `json_escape` sibling, and `emit_text_fold_program`'s `static_per_element += max_len`), but nothing enforced N at runtime, so the FILL pass wrote the ACTUAL bytes — recovered by `strlen` — into a buffer sized from the DECLARED bound. `tag : text [..8]` with `out = concat("[", b.tag, "]")` reserved 16 bytes and copied whatever the caller passed; the overflow was **controlled**, the attacker's fill byte landing in the process exit code (`'A'`→65, `'B'`→66, `'Z'`→90, `'0'`→48). Perversely, an UNBOUNDED field was SAFE (it takes the runtime-strlen sizing path) — **declaring the bound is what opted a program into the unchecked static path**, the exact inversion of "declarations add rigor". The same defect was REMOTELY reachable through the compiler-owned `HttpRequest` bounds (`method : text [..8]`, `path : text [..256]`), which `emit_http_parse_method_path` never compared against: measured, a 3001-byte path echoed ~2740 attacker-chosen bytes past the buffer on `examples/echo_path.verbose`. Fix: new `emit_text_bound_check` (a `max+1`-budget `repne scasb` — bounded, so an adversarial multi-MB argument cannot force a scan the declaration says we never do) at **all 11** argv/stdin text field-load sites across the six prologue emitters, sharing the existing `resource_abort_patches` sys_exit(1) tail; plus two 20-byte `len = rbx - start; cmp; jg fail` guards in the HTTP parse, routed to the existing close-connection-without-response path (per-request fail-closed — an over-long path must not be a remote kill switch). One check per prologue loop covers argv, `--stdin`, `--stdin-raw` and `--stream`, because all four converge on the same `mov rdi, [r13+r14*8]` loops. **+23 B per bounded Text input field** (+16 once more if the program had no abort tail yet); **+40 B per dynamic HTTP service**. Programs with no bounded text input field compile byte-for-byte identically — 146 of 245 corpus binaries unchanged, and no binary changed size-identically-but-bytes-differently. | +23 B/bounded-text-field; +40 B/http-service | `alert.verbose` (+23 B), `echo_path.verbose` (1263 → 1303 B) |
| (security) | **`HttpRequest.body`'s declared bound now TRACKS `max_request`** (2026-08-30) — it was a hardcoded `[..4096]` in BOTH synthesis sites (`builtin_http_request` in `src/verifier.rs`, `http_request_builtin_concept_native` in `src/native.rs`), while the verifier's own doc comment on that function said body is *"capped by the service's `max_request` at runtime"* and `verify_service` bounds `max_request` only by `!= 0`. So `max_request : 65536` carried a declared 4096-byte bound on a field the wire can fill with ~65500 bytes: a **FALSE declaration**, and a text field's `[..N]` is EXPLOITED for compile-time buffer sizing (`emit_concat_to_buffer_impl`: `static_total += N` for a bounded Text field, `static_total += 2*N` for `json_escape` of one) — the exact shape of the 2026-08-05 argv-controlled overflow. **It was NOT reachable, and the claim that it was statically exploited is wrong**: `req.body` is registered in `http_text_bindings` whenever it is referenced (`uses_body` covers handler logic, handler lets and every log block), so `classify_concat_arg` returns `BoundText` and the RUNTIME-sized path takes over — measured, a 60000-byte body through `concat("[", req.body, "]")` at `max_request : 65536` returns 60002 correct bytes. The one arm that DOES read the concept range is `json_escape`'s `inner_static_max`, which matches `Expr::Field` **without consulting `text_bindings`** — and that shape dies in the fill pass with `json_escape inner field 'body' has no rbp slot`. A latent landmine, not a live hole: wire body's rbp slot into the json_escape fill (a plausible slice) and the sizing silently becomes `2*4096` against an unbounded runtime length. Fixed by DERIVING the bound rather than by adding a guard, so the declaration is true BY CONSTRUCTION — the body is a suffix of a buffer `read(client_fd, buf, max_request)` already caps, which is why it needs no runtime check, unlike `method`/`path` whose bounds are independent constants and are enforced explicitly in `emit_http_parse_method_path`. Native uses the COMPILED service's exact `max_request` (threaded through `emit_http10_dynamic_bytes` → `emit_handler_to_slots` / `emit_http_parse_method_path`); the verifier's concept is program-wide while `max_request` is per-service, so it takes the MAXIMUM over every Http10 service — the tightest program-wide bound true for all of them, ≥ native's and therefore only ever loose in the safe direction (and identical for a single-service program, which is every example and the only shape `--run <service>` compiles). | +0 B (measured across the whole corpus) | `counter_service.verbose` (975 B, unchanged); pinned by `http_request_body_bound_tracks_max_request` |
| A (slice 5.1b) | **First runtime-recursive native binary.** Lifts slice 5.1a's "arg must be Ident(input)" by also accepting `Record(input_concept, { field: expr })` at the recursive call site. Caller emits `<eval expr → rax> ; mov rdi, rax ; call <label>` — same call/ret target as slice 5.1a, but the value passed in rdi is a fresh derived value instead of the pass-through field. Worked example: classic factorial — `out = if n.v == 0 then 1 else n.v * fact(N { v: n.v - 1 })`. Composed with the runtime bounds-check: `v : number [0, 10]` enforces recursion depth ≤ 11 at runtime. Together, slices 5.0 + 5.1a + bounds-check + 5.1b are the audit-defensible recursion floor: a binary compiles iff verifier accepts, runs iff input passes the declared bound, and bounded depth gives a defensible "max stack" claim. Phase C structural recursion will replace the bound-by-input-range argument with a mechanical termination proof. | ~800 B | `factorial.verbose::fact` (802 B; computes `n!` for n ∈ [0, 10]) |
| A (slice 5.2) | **Text return from a recursive callable.** Two-register System-V-style return: `(rax = ptr, rdx = len)`. Body grammar: chained if/else where leaves are either text literals (embedded inline via jmp-over-data) or self-calls. The literal data lives in the binary's executable region — `rax` points at it after the leaf load, and the pointer survives the callable's epilogue because it doesn't dereference frame memory. Recursion threads (rax, rdx) up through `ret`s; the _start loop's final write picks up the converged pair and emits to stdout via `sys_write(1, rax, rdx)`. Concat and heap-allocated text return are explicitly NOT in scope — that's a later slice once the stack-budget design lands. The constraint keeps the audit story flat: returned pointers point at static data, never at frame-local buffers. Worked example: `recursive_label.verbose::label_of` recurses to the base case (v == 0) returning `"done"`, then propagates the same (rax, rdx) pair through every parent frame. | ~740 B | `recursive_label.verbose::label_of` (739 B) |
| A (slice 5.3) | **Multi-field input via pointer-in-rdi ABI (Option A).** First Verbose binary that passes a multi-field record across `call`/`ret` via a struct pointer. ABI: rdi = pointer to a fields struct laid out on the caller's stack below rsp, one i64 per field in declaration order.
